Request: 
In all interrogatories, all requests for workpapers or supporting calculations shall be construed as 
requesting any Word, Excel or other computer spreadsheet models in original electronic format 
with all formulae intact and unlocked. 
1. Provide a detailed explanation of the length of lag time when plant and property additions 
become part of the assessment used to calculate property taxes, starting from when plant and 
property is added to when it is initially taxed. 
2. Provide a detailed description of the step-by-step process by which KEDNY obtains 
obsolescence awards from New York State Office of Real Property Services (ORPTS), from 
when KEDNY first filed the obsolescence requests, until the obsolescence percentages 
presented in Exhibit__(SSP-1) and Exhibit__(SSP-2) are applied to property tax assessments 
in their respective calendar years. 
3. For the following question for KEDNY, refer to Exhibit__(SSP-1), Schedule 2, and 
Exhibit__(SSP-2), Schedule 2.  
a.
Provide a complete copy of each economic obsolescence and/or functional 
obsolescence filing KEDNY made with ORPTS for calendar years 2015 through 2018.  
Include copies of the determination letters received and any supporting documentation 
for the filings.  
b. Provide an explanation for why ORPTS has not yet awarded KEDNY obsolescence 
for 2019. 
4. For KEDNY, refer to Exhibit__(RRP-5), Schedule 1, p. 3.  Provide the list of actual property 
taxes for CY 2015, 2016, and 2017 in the same format as the list of actual property taxes for 
CY 2018.

Form 103 
5. For KEDNY, refer to Exhibit__(RRP-5), Schedule 1, p. 3.  Provide the list of actual property 
taxes for CY 2015, 2016, and 2017 in the same format as described in Question #4 above, but

Response: 
1.
KEDNY reports its assets to the New York Office of Real Property Tax Services 
(“ORPTS”) on a calendar-year basis.  The Company is required to report its assets as of 
December 31, 2018, including any assets added throughout calendar year 2018, by April 30, 
2019.  ORPTS will analyze and address questions regarding the filings throughout 2019 and 
determine the value of the special franchise assets in each municipality.  ORPTS will report the 
values to each municipality, and the municipalities will add them to the tentative assessment roll 
that most will publish by May 1, 2020.   The final assessment roll will be published by July 1, 
2020.  School taxes are generally the first taxes levied on the new assessment roll, and although 
their tax year begins July 1st, the bills are not issued until September or later.
2.
KEDNY is required to annually file for obsolescence by April 30th for the prior 
calendar year data to be considered for any kind of relief.  ORPTS reviews and analyzes the 
application, responds to the applicant with any questions regarding the filing.  Once all questions 
and issues have been resolved, ORPTS will submit a letter of determination sometime between 
September and October of that year.  ORPTS uses their determined level of obsolescence to 
prepare the value of the Company’s special franchise property and provides those values to the 
municipalities.  Each municipality then equalizes the ORPTS value(s) and places them on the 
tentative assessment roll on May 1st of the following year.  Property owners are given the 
opportunity to contest their assessment during the month of May.  If there are any issues, the 
municipalities work with the property owner to resolve the differences.  All changes and 
adjustments to the tentative tax roll must be completed by July 1.  Taxing authorities then levy 
taxes based on these values for their tax year that begins on or after July 1st.  The process takes 
about 15 months from the point of filing the obsolescence application in April until the taxes are 
levied on values in July of the following year as outlined in the following example. 
Process Step 
Date 
Apply to ORPTS using calendar 2018 data 
April 30, 2019 
ORPTS Determination Letter Issued 
September-October 2019 
Municipalities Issue Final 2020 Assessment Rolls 
July 1, 2020 
First Taxes Levied on 2020 Assessment Roll  
July 1, 2020

Please see the attachments as outlined below for the obsolescence filings and 
determination letters requested as well as those for the 2019 assessment roll.

Attachment 1

This memorandum, and the papers filed concurrently herewith, constitute the application of 
THE BROOKLYN UNION COMPANY, a National Grid Company (hereinafter, “Brooklyn Union”) for 
obsolescence (“Application”) relative to the valuation of its special franchise real property, or advisory 
appraisals of its real property not located in the public right-of-way, by the State Board or State Office of 
Real Property Tax Services (“State Board” or “ORPTS”) for the 2019 final assessment rolls, and thereafter. 
I. 
Introduction and Summary 
 
More specifically, Brooklyn Union seeks recognition of the following corrections, adjustments 
and forms of obsolescence: 
A.  Calculating Reproduction Cost New:  Handy-Whitman Trend Index Correction 
B. Physical Depreciation:  Trend Ceiling Adjustment 
C.  Functional Obsolescence 
1. Excess Capacity (Super-adequacy) 
2. Excess Capital Costs for Pipe  
D. Economic Obsolescence 
1. Adjustment and Comparison to Market - Derived Weighted Average Cost of Capital 
 
Each claim is separate and distinct.  As such, each claimed adjustment is additive, not duplicative.  
Simply, there is no overlap or double-counting by these distinct and separate corrections, adjustment 
and forms of obsolescence.   
 
The State of New York Office of Real Property Tax Services is the sole determinant of the special 
franchise assessments that are used by the local municipalities and taxing authorities on their final 
assessment roles.  See Real Property Tax Law (“RPTL”) Article 6.  In addition, ORPTS is authorized to 
provide advisory appraisals to municipalities in limited circumstances.  See RPTL §1544 (permits ORPTS 
to provide advisory appraisals upon request for taxable public utility properties).  ORPTS offers its 
valuation services with respect to utility property not located in the right-of-way (i.e., non-special 
franchise property) pursuant to 9 NYCRR §195-2.  In recognition of ORPTS’ dual authority, this 
application requests that ORPTS incorporate the identified obsolescence (set forth herein) into its 
determination of Brooklyn Union’s special franchise assessments and advisory appraisals. 
 
Brooklyn Union reserves the right to apply other methodologies and factors to calculate its 
functional and economic obsolescence.  These additional methods and factors are likely to result in an 
even higher computed obsolescence. 
             Appendix A 
       Page 7 of 107


--- Page 17 ---
5 
 
II. 
The Problems and Requested Solutions 
A. 
Calculating Reproduction Cost New:  Handy Whitman Trend Index Correction 
 
The Handy-Whitman Index weightings (e.g., basic materials, labor, and equipment) do not 
provide adjustments for modern improvements in construction standards. As a result, inefficiencies in 
the trends are captured in historic indices dating back to 1912. Using multiple linear regression analysis 
we were able to determine a suitable “modern” weighting of labor and material prices to apply to 
historic prices from 1912-1972.  In applying this regression analysis, we compared the HW Index by FERC 
Account against the CPI Urban New York/New Jersey and, separately, HW Material Prices as published in 
the Handy-Whitman Bulletins. Based on the results of this regression analysis, adjustments were made 
to the HW Index by FERC Account and vintage.  Using the published Handy-Whitman indices to calculate 
Reproduction Cost New (“ReproCN”) without these adjustments results in functional obsolescence due 
to excess capital costs attributable to the index weightings’ failure to take into account modern 
construction standards and materials. 
 
These adjustments to the HW Index (and effective remapping) should be adopted by ORPTS for 
determining ReproCN for both Brooklyn Union’s special franchise property and advisory appraisals for 
any city, town or village in 2019.  See Exhibit A.   
B. 
Physical Depreciation:  Trend Ceiling Adjustment 
 
ORPTS’s administrative procedures provide for trending original cost to compute the 
reproduction cost new (“RCN”).  To compute depreciation, ORPTS applies a “Percent Good Factor” floor 
of five (5%) percent that results in assets increasing in value after reaching the percent good floor of 5%.  
To eliminate this impact to such assets’ values, we request the implementation of a trend ceiling, which 
caps the trend factor for computing RCN at the point that is 95% of the total service life.  
 
These adjustments should be adopted by ORPTS for determining RCN for both Brooklyn Union’s 
special franchise property and advisory appraisals for any city, town or village in 2019.   
C. 
Functional Obsolescence - Excess Capital Cost: Cast Iron versus Plastic Pipe 
 
We also request that ORPTS apply an adjustment for functional obsolescence due to excess 
capital costs of Brooklyn Union’s system piping.  Functional Obsolescence is defined as the loss in value 
of a property caused by inefficiencies or inadequacies of the property itself, when compared to a more 
efficient or less costly replacement property that new technology has developed.  Symptoms such as 
excess operating cost, excess capital cost, overcapacity, inadequacy, and lack of utility suggest the 
presence of functional obsolescence. 
 
Functional obsolescence for the Brooklyn Union is revealed through its excess capital costs or 
the difference between an asset’s reproduction and replacement cost.  This type of functional 
obsolescence occurs when a lower capital investment is required to obtain a more economical new 
asset of the same utility.  Matter of Consolidated Edison Company of New York, Inc. v. City of New York, 
Index No. 8564/98 (Sup.Ct. Kings Co. 2004) (Hon. Michael L. Pesce), aff’d 33A.D.3d 915 (2nd Dep’t 2006), 
             Appendix A 
       Page 8 of 107


--- Page 18 ---
6 
 
aff’d 8 N.Y.3d 591 (2007).  It is less costly and more economical to replace the pipe in the Brooklyn 
Union’s system with other materials rather than the existing materials (e.g., replace low pressure cast 
iron pipe with high pressure and smaller diameter plastic pipe).  
The functional obsolescence factor in determining the system wide value of these assets for both 
Brooklyn Union’s special franchise property and advisory appraisals for any city, town or village in 2019 
is at least 21.97% for Gas Distribution Mains and at least 15.02% for Gas Distribution Services.  See 
Exhibit B. 
D. 
 Economic Obsolescence:  Market-Derived Weighted Average Cost of Capital (“WACC”) 
 
The current ORPTS model for calculating economic obsolescence fails to consider either a 
market based analysis or that the utility market is not limited to the State of New York, resulting in an 
overvaluation of Brooklyn Union’s special franchise property.  As a result, the ORPTS model fails to 
capture the true economic obsolescence influencing the property.  A market-based approach using a 
weighted-average cost of capital is the appropriate methodology for calculating economic obsolescence. 
 
The economic obsolescence factor that should be used by ORPTS in determining the system-
wide value of all assets for both Brooklyn Union’s special franchise property and advisory appraisals for 
any city, town or village in 2019 is at least 64.48%.  See Exhibit C & D. 
III. 
Applicable Law 
 
This Application is made in accordance with the rules and regulations of the State Board, 9 
NYCRR §197-2.8 (see also 9 NYCRR Part 195 for advisory appraisals), as well as the administrative 
procedures adopted by the State Board for special franchise valuations. 
 
Brooklyn Union’s special franchise property must all be valued by the reproduction cost new less 
depreciation methodology (“ReproCNLD”) (the cost approach).  As recognized by the State Board in its 
regulations, in applying ReproCNLD there are three forms of depreciation – physical depreciation, 
functional obsolescence and economic obsolescence.  See Procedures of ORPS, Special Franchise 
Assessments and Values:   City, Town and Village Assessment Rolls (Final November 2001) (“ORPS 
Procedures”), §2.4-2.6.  See also, Long Island Lighting Company v. Assessor for Town of Brookhaven, 
Index Nos. 76/15628, et seq. (Sup. Ct. Nassau Co.), aff’d, 202 A.D.2d 32, 616 N.Y.S.2d 375 (2d Dep’t 
1994); AMERICAN INSTITUTE OF REAL ESTATE APPRAISERS, The Appraisal of Real Estate (10th Ed. 1992), p. 312.  
The State Board routinely and unilaterally considers physical depreciation in determining the value of 
special franchise property and in conducting its advisory appraisals (as the same valuation methodology 
is used for both) (mainly incurable physical depreciation; see 9 NYCRR §197-2.8; ORPS Procedures, §2.4).  
Conversely, for functional and economic obsolescence, the State Board requires an application from the 
taxpayer before it considers either.  See 9 NYCRR §197-2.8; ORPS Procedure, §§2.5 and 2.6. 
 
Pursuant to New York case law, functional obsolescence “is a loss of value resulting from defects 
in design.  It can also be caused by changes that, over time, have made some aspect of a structure, such 
as its materials or design, obsolete by current standards” (emphasis added).  See Appraisal Institute, 
             Appendix A 
       Page 9 of 107


--- Page 19 ---
7 
 
The Appraisal of Real Estate, Tenth Edition, ©1992, p. 352.  Functional obsolescence is the “loss of 
operating efficiency.”  Onondaga County Water District v. Board of Assessors, 39 N.Y.2d 601, 605, 385 
N.Y.S.2d 13, 17 (1976) (citing, Valuation of Property, Volume I, pp. 187-188); Piazza v. Assessor of Town 
of Porter, 16 A.D.2d 863 (4th Dep’t 1962) (functional obsolescence includes “inadequacies of design”).  
That is, “[f]unctional obsolescence includes the loss in value within the property as a result of 
development of new technology,…changes in design, materials or processes resulting [in] overcapacity, 
inadequacy, excess construction, lack of utility or excess fixed or variable operating costs” (emphasis 
added).  Long Island Lighting Company v. Assessor for Town of Brookhaven, supra, slip op. at p. 33. 
 
Functional obsolescence is demonstrated by comparing the existent construction, design, 
materials and technologies of existing assets, for example, with the design and technological advances 
employed in a state-of-the-art facility or equipment.  Long Island Lighting Company v. Assessor for Town 
of Brookhaven, supra; Matter of Consolidated Edison Company of New York, Inc. v. City of New York, 
Index No. 8564/98 (Sup.Ct. Kings Co. 2004)(Hon. Michael L. Pesce), aff’d 33A.D.3d 915 (2nd Dep’t 2006), 
aff’d 8 N.Y.3d 591 (2007). 
 
With respect to economic obsolescence, it has been defined as “the diminished utility of a 
structure due to negative influences emanating from outside the building, is usually incurable on the 
part of the owner [and], landlord or tenant.”  See Appraisal Institute, The Appraisal of Real Estate, Tenth 
Edition, ©1992, p.358.  For example, if a law imposes a duty on a public utility corporation that 
constrains its ability to achieve an economic return on its real and personal property, that statutory 
constraint constitutes an outside or external force that represents external or economic obsolescence.  
See The Appraisal of Real Estate, supra, pp.352, 358. 
IV. 
Critical Factors to Consider 
 
In applying these depreciation and obsolescence factors to this Application, ORPTS must 
consider, understand and apply the following: 
1. The history and relevant facts concerning the Handy Whitman Trend Index; 
2. The relevant facts concerning the NY ORPTS Physical Depreciation Calculation and Percent Good 
Factors and the effect of applying a five percent floor; 
3. That the change in technology, design, construction standards and materials in modern natural 
gas  pipelines has created functional obsolescence; 
4. That market value for utility property requires consideration of market-required return rates to 
identify and calculate economic obsolescence; and 
5. The relevant utility market for comparison and calculation of economic obsolescence extends 
beyond the borders of New York State.

A. 
History and Relevant Facts Concerning The Handy Whitman Trend Index 
 
The Handy-Whitman factors were first developed in 1914 to measure cost trends at utilities by 
account across 6 regions of the United States.  The components of the indices primarily include a 
materials component and a labor component.  The source of the components and the associated 
weightings are proprietary and closely held by the index publishers.  According to Whitman Requardt & 
Associates, the publishers of the index, “these data are reviewed continuously, and weightings and 
components are revised as required.”1   
 
However, our research has shown that the index weightings have not been updated in more 
than 40 years. Moreover, any changes in weightings are not adjusted in prior years; older vintages will 
not reflect improvements in construction standards.   The publishers concede this point in The Handy-
Whitman Index of Public Utility Construction Costs, Bulletin No. 172, November 2010: 
 
“State-of-the-art changes often affect costs independently of inflation.  New regulatory   
 
and environmental requirements, changes in work rules and improved design standards,  
 
for instance, increase construction costs even though the price of wages, materials and   
 
equipment may be static. Trended construction costs will not reflect such changes.”2 
 
Reproduction cost indices like the Handy Whitman Index are also used in other industries; AUS 
Consultants, for example, publishes a series of trend indices for the telephone industry, called the AUS 
Telephone Plant Index. Like the Handy Whitman Index, ORPTS applies this index to compute RCN for the 
telecommunication industry.  
 
Unlike the Handy Whitman index, the AUS Telephone index contains a “modernization 
adjustment” – namely, a correction factor applied on older vintages to remove overhead that was 
disallowed after an FCC ruling in 1973. Essentially, the AUS Telephone Index publishers adjusted the 
weights of prior years to match modern standards.  The Handy Whitman publishers make no such 
“modernization” adjustment on any of its indices. 
 
As previously mentioned, all trend indices contain a materials component and labor component. 
The exact weightings are closely held by publishers.  However, we were able to reverse engineer the 
weightings by using historic Handy Whitman materials prices and a regional Consumer Price Index 
published by the Bureau of Labor Statistics.  Our analysis concluded that not only have the weightings of 
the index not changed since 1973, but the weightings for vintages older than 1973 are significantly 
HIGHER than for those in place after 1973. 
B. 
Relevant Facts Concerning the NY ORPTS Physical Depreciation Calculation and 
Percent Good Factors 
 
According to the ORPTS procedures for valuing special franchise properties, a ReproCNLD 
approach is used whereby original costs are trended, using the Handy-Whitman Index (“HW”), and

subtracting physical depreciation and functional and/or economic obsolescence to estimate market 
value.  In calculating the depreciation/obsolescence, ORPTS limits the total deductions for physical, 
functional, and economic depreciation such that the resultant value indication cannot be less than 5% of 
the ReproCN; thus, setting a depreciation floor for the assets being valued.  
 
In calculating physical depreciation, ORPTS utilizes service lives and net salvage percentages for 
each of the various utility property accounts defined by the New York Public Service Commission 
(“NYPSC”) to establish a percent good factor that is applied to the ReproCN new as follows: 
1.00 – net salvage percent x (age of asset / service life) = Percent Good Factor* 
*the Percent Good Factor cannot be lower than 5% 
 
By trending original cost and applying the Percent Good Factor floor, the market value of assets 
still in service beyond their determined service lives will actually increase in value the longer they stay in 
service.3  To eliminate this effect, we request the implementation of a trend ceiling, which caps the 
trend factor once the asset reaches a depreciation factor of 95 percent (i.e., a total service life of 40 
years will have a capped trend factor corresponding to an age of 38 years (40 x .95 = 38)).  
 
Illustrating this change graphically, Graph II-2.1 shows the standard HW trends for certain 
electric utility categories.  Graph II-2.2 shows the same electric utility category trends capped at 95% of 
their ORPTS service lives.  As the graph differences demonstrate, by capping category trends at 95% of 
ORPTS service lives, the artificial and erroneous valuation increases from non-capped trending is 
precluded.   
 
The necessity for a cap is further mandated by the HW Index’s propensity to over state 
reproduction cost new, as illustrated in “A” above.

D. 
Changes in Technology with Respect to Cast Iron versus Plastic Pipe Gas  Pipelines Has 
Resulted in Functional Obsolescence 
 
The technological advancements of steel and plastic pipe have resulted in functional 
obsolescence inherent in outdated cast iron mains and services.  Up until the 1950s, cast iron pipe was 
the material of choice for natural gas delivery.4  Pipe was connected with jute, a plant fiber that makes 
an airtight seal when moist.  The jute was then sealed, most commonly with lead.  Over time, the 
methane dried the jute creating leakage of the natural gas from the cast iron pipe.  New technologies 
drastically reduced the amount of natural gas lost from leaky joints.5.6 
 
After 1950, cast iron was replaced with welded steel pipe.  The joints in steel pipe do not leak, 
and the pipe is less expensive to manufacture and install as well.  Steel found a home in the high 
                                                          
4 American Gas Association, http://www.aga.org/Kc/aboutnaturalgas/additional/NGDeliverySystemMaterials.htm 
5 National Energy Technology Laboratory (NETL), http://www.netl.doe.gov/technologies/oil-
gas/NaturalGas/Projects_n/TDS/TD/T&D_A_41316SealingJoints.html 
6 Pipeline & Gas Journal, New Measurement Data Has Implications for Quantifying Natural Gas Losses from Cast 
Iron Distribution Mains; Carey Bylin, Luigi Cassab, Adilson Cazarini, Danilo Ori, Don Robinson, and Doug Sechler, 
September 2009 Col. 235 No. 9 
             Appendix A 
       Page 14 of 107


--- Page 24 ---
12 
 
pressure sections of the natural gas transmission network due to these superior attributes.  Some low 
pressure distribution mains are steel; however, a new technology has replaced the small diameter 
piping.7 
 
In the last thirty years, plastic pipe has become the choice material for installation at pressures 
less than 100 pounds per square inch.  Plastic pipe is more flexible and costs much less to install.  It is 
available in lengths much longer than that of cast iron; thus, lowering the time needed for installation 
due to joint construction and massive trenching.8 
It is worth noting that we are not requesting that ORPTS apply an adjustment for functional 
obsolescence due to excess capacity inherent in the design and construction of Brooklyn Union’s system 
piping, despite the evidence as well as basic appraisal and valuation methodology and procedures 
regarding the use of a Cost Approach to value.  Functional Obsolescence is defined as the loss in value of 
a property caused by inefficiencies or inadequacies of the property itself, when compared to a more 
efficient or less costly replacement property that new technology has developed.  As previously noted, 
symptoms such as excess operating cost, excess capital cost, overcapacity, inadequacy, and lack of utility 
suggest the presence of functional obsolescence. 
 
The Cost Approach recognizes that a prudent investor will not pay more for a property than the 
cost of acquiring a substitute property of equivalent utility.9  The Cost Approach must take into 
consideration all physical depreciation, as well as any functional and external obsolescence that is 
present in the subject assets, as would a prudent investor in arriving at a reasonable estimate of market 
value.  It is our understanding that the cost approach as applied herein is consistent with the value in 
continued use premise required in New York. Estimates of the cost to construct a substitute property 
may be developed by either of two techniques: 
Reproduction Cost – The estimated cost to construct, at current prices, an exact duplicate of the 
asset being appraised, using the same materials, construction standards, design, layout and 
quality of workmanship, and embodying all the property’s deficiencies, super-adequacies, 
and/or obsolescence.  
Replacement Cost - The cost to replace an existing property with a property of equivalent utility 
as of a particular date. The replacement cost concept is the most meaningful as far as the 
principle of substitution is concerned. 
To estimate Fair Market Value, Reproduction Cost New (“ReproCN”), or Replacement Cost New 
(“ReplCN”), each type of cost would typically be adjusted to reflect depreciation inherent in the Subject 
Assets (to the extent such depreciation is not already embedded in ReproCN or ReplCN).  Depreciation 
for valuation purposes is defined by the American Society of Appraisers (“ASA”) as the “estimated loss in 
value of an asset, compared with the new asset; appraisal depreciation measures value inferiority that is 
                                                          
7 American Gas Association, http://www.aga.org/Kc/aboutnaturalgas/additional/NGDeliverySystemMaterials.htm 
8 American Gas Association, http://www.aga.org/Kc/aboutnaturalgas/additional/NGDeliverySystemMaterials.htm 
9 American Society of Appraisers, Valuing Machinery and Equipment:  The Fundamentals of Appraising Machinery 
and Technical Assets, Second Edition, 2000, page 63. 
             Appendix A 
       Page 15 of 107


--- Page 25 ---
13 
 
caused by a combination of physical, functional obsolescence, and economic (or external) 
obsolescence.”10   
In most cases, physical depreciation can be quantified using an age-life methodology, whereby 
an asset depreciates at a quantifiable rate each year of its anticipated useful life.  Functional 
obsolescence is quantified by comparing the functionality of the Subject Assets to the functionality of 
similar new assets.  Economic obsolescence is generally quantified by an analysis of the valuation of the 
entire business in which the asset is utilized.  If the cash flows of a company are not adequate to support 
the value of the asset as developed using the Cost Approach, economic obsolescence may be present.  
The ReplCN is generally the proper starting point for developing an opinion of value using the 
cost approach11.  If appropriate information is available, ReplCN is the preferred choice for older assets, 
specialty-use assets, assets that are likely to suffer from functional/technical obsolescence, or assets for 
which the fixed asset records are deemed unreliable.  A ReplCN analysis eliminates the potential to 
incorrectly ascribe value to “ghost assets” (i.e., a situation where assets remain in the fixed asset records 
after they have been retired from service); a ReproCN study assumes all assets on the ledger are in-use 
and thus, ascribes value to all assets. 
Conceptually, the ideal ReplCN for a subject asset would assume the plant would be rebuilt 
“Greenfield”.  This Greenfield approach aims to replace the utility of the existing plant or assets, but not 
its inefficiencies, inadequacies, duplication of assets, use of older technologies, excess capital cost of 
staged build-outs, and so on.12  
 
The current gas system has capacity limitations that are basically the result of age and the 
technologies available at the time the system was constructed.  For example, repairs to the system are 
made with the same size plastic or steel pipe since new or replacement cast iron pipe is prohibited 
under Title 16 NYCRR Part 255.57.  This results in a system that has cast iron pipe interspersed with 
plastic and steel.  The system’s actual operating pressure is limited to low pressure because of the 
pressure limitations of the cast iron pipe.   We could have obtained a significantly greater capacity from 
our system but for the presence of cast iron pipe.  This is an example of functional obsolescence. 
 
 There is another form of functional obsolescence involving gas supply and design capacity.  
Until about 1953, gas supplied to customers was manufactured within the franchise.   The heat content 
of manufactured gas was approximately 500 to 550 Btu per cubic foot, which necessitated the use of 
predominantly 4" and 6" mains operating at low pressure.   In the early 1950's interstate pipelines

functionality of the existing plant.  Total capitalized costs include the cost of materials, direct labor, direct 
overhead and the capitalized cost of interest or imputed charges for interest which are necessary to make the 
property operational.

brought large quantities of natural gas from the Gulf Area to the New York area.  Because the heat 
content of the new gas supply was approximately 1010 to 1050 Btu per cubic foot, or about double the 
heat content of the manufactured gas, the capacity of the existing system effectively doubled.  As such, 
the existing piping network was generally oversized.  It should also be noted that the introduction of 
natural gas from proposed Liquefied Natural Gas terminals and the development of a liquid commodity 
market will further increase the BTU content of natural gas, thereby adding excess capacity to the 
existing system. 
Additionally, natural gas can be delivered at pressures that are high enough to eliminate the 
need for compression. Brooklyn Union simply needed to install regulators to reduce the pressure before 
putting the gas into the existing distribution systems.  Had this source of gas been available during the 
initial installation of the system, a smaller diameter, high-pressure system would have been installed, 
instead of the larger diameter, low-pressure mains that were in fact installed.  
The presentation of value in this report uses ReproCN as a starting point to determine the 
market value of the subject assets. It is important to note that further valuation studies performed on 
these subject assets based on ReplCN may offer significantly different conclusions of the value of the 
property, since obsolescence in the existing subject assets would be more easily quantified in this 
manner.  While Brooklyn Union could have measured all forms of obsolescence and depreciation, as 
promulgated by the traditional application of an appropriate cost approach methodology and required 
by New York law, we only address and present the functional obsolescence resulting from excess capital 
costs for pipe as part of this analysis.  We focus the efforts herein only in direct correlation to ORPTS 
traditional methodology of arriving at value conclusions for the subject assets through the application of 
ORPS cost approach methodology.   
E. 
Market Value for Utility Property Requires Consideration of Market-Required Return 
Rates to Identify and Calculate Economic Obsolescence 
 
 
Economic Obsolescence is identified as any value loss that arises from sources outside of the 
property itself.  For regulated utilities, rate base regulation is the most common source of economic 
obsolescence.  Rates of return allowed in rate base regulation frequently fail to keep up with market-
required return rates, thus creating a shortfall between the achieved return rate and the market return 
rate.  The effect of this income shortfall is to create a loss in market value that is called “economic 
obsolescence.”  
 
The next question is, “How should we quantify economic obsolescence?”  Although ORPTS has 
set forth its preferred methodology for valuing special franchise property in its administrative 
procedures, the N.Y. Const. of 1938, art. XVI, § 2 provides the ultimate guidance.  “Assessments shall in 
no case exceed full value.”  Id.  “Full value” has long been equated with “market value.”  People ex rel. 
Parklin Operating Corp. v. Miller 287 N.Y. 126, 38 N.E.2d 465 (N.Y. 1941).  “The statutory test of the full 
value of property is the price at which the property would sell under ordinary circumstances.”  Id.   
             Appendix A 
       Page 17 of 107


--- Page 27 ---
15 
 
 
Using the State Board’s preferred methodology for calculating economic obsolescence in this 
instance would result in an assessment that exceeds market value because it fails to consider market-
based expectations of return on assets.  This approach is widely recognized as the correct methodology 
for calculating economic obsolescence.  The Office of Real Property Tax Services (“ORPTS”) has itself 
employed a similar market-based approach for calculating economic obsolescence in an appraisal it filed 
in a dispute between New York State Electric and Gas (“NYSEG”) and the State of New York.  In that 
dispute, ORPTS’s appraisal (prepared by American Appraisal Associates) calculated return on equity 
(“ROE”) for publicly traded utility companies inside New York State and return on equity (“ROE”) for 
publicly traded utility companies outside the State (using Value Line Investment Survey).  ORPTS then 
compared the ROE achieved by NYSEG with an “estimated market required return” for both sets of 
publicly traded utility companies.  In doing so, ORPTS recognized the market was not limited to New 
York State. 
F. 
The Relevant Utility Market for Comparison and Calculation of Economic Obsolescence 
Extends Beyond the Borders of New York State 
 
The economic obsolescence valuation methodology routinely relied upon by ORPTS compares 
Brooklyn Union’s return on assets with returns earned by other utility companies within New York State.  
New York courts have acknowledged, however, that when determining the market value of property, it 
is necessary and appropriate to look outside the State of New York.  See E.g., Long Island Lighting Co. v. 
Assessor for Town of Brookhaven, 202 A.D.2d 32, 616 N.Y.S.2d 375 (N.Y. App. Div. 1994); Niagara 
Mohawk Power Corporation v. City of Cohoes, 280 A.D.2d 724, 727-278 (3d Dep’t 2000), lv denied, 96 
N.Y.2d 719 (2001).  Furthermore, ORPTS staff, giving guidance to taxpayers on economic obsolescence 
claims, acknowledges that “[t]he loss in value [due to economic obsolescence] can be measured several 
ways, but adequate documentation must be provided.”  See Office of Real Property Services Website, 
Valuing and Assessing Real Property, Inventory, Additional Information on Economic Obsolescence 
Requests, available at: 
http://www.tax.ny.gov/research/property/valuation/economicobsolescence.htm (last visited 
4/09/2018).   
 
Accordingly, in estimating the market value of Brooklyn Union’s property, it is necessary to 
survey the appropriate market in order to gather all the relevant data.  Investor services, such as Value 
Line Investment Survey, also indicate that Brooklyn Union’s market is larger than the State of New York.  
Value Line Investment Survey recognizes a U.S. natural gas distribution industry.  For all the foregoing 
reasons, the economic obsolescence valuation methodology employed by ORPTS should rely upon a 
comparison of its achieved rate of return on assets with the current market cost of capital for utility 
companies in the U.S. natural gas distribution industry.  Furthermore, the U.S. natural gas distribution 
industry must be examined in order to determine market return rates against which the achieved return 
rates can be measured.

V. 
Computation of Obsolescence 
A. 
Handy Whitman Trend Factors Must Be Adjusted In Order to Determine Reproduction 
Cost New 
 
A total of 39 indices in the Gas and Electric Utility schedules were selected in our analysis. The 
adjustments to the Handy Whitman indices were devised by performing a multiple linear regression 
analysis on the pre-1973 and the post-1973 portions of each index. The materials used in the analysis of 
each index were selected through a combination of engineering expertise and retirement data from 
National Grid. To minimize statistical error, no more than 3 materials for each index were selected to 
include in the analysis. Each input was normalized; i.e. converted to a base value of 1.0 based off the 
July 2010 value. Finally, the analysis was conducted with a y-intercept of zero to fully account for all 
measurable sources of price changes. For a single-material index, the modeled function would resemble 
the following: 
 
 
The data was then regressed and results were plotted on an Analysis of Variance (ANOVA) table. 
Regression results were evaluated for a minimum adjusted R square of 95%. Additionally, all results 
were checked for a high F score to determine the fitness of the overall model. Finally, each predictor 
variable in the analysis was evaluated for a minimum p-value of 0.2; anything larger was deemed 
statistically insignificant and discarded. The regression would be repeated until all these conditions were 
met. 
 
After regression results were performed for pre-1973 and post-1973 vintages, a revised set of 
indices were rebuilt by multiplying the post-1973 weightings on each input to the pre-1973 input prices. 
To simplify the analysis, the post-1973 index numbers were left unchanged. The two series of indices 
(pre-1973 prices with post-1973 weightings, and the post-1973 index values) were then combined to 
form a brand new set of indices. Each index was then un-normalized to arrive at a trend schedule that 
resembles the published Handy Whitman index, but with new “modernization” adjustments. 
B. 
Handy Whitman Trend Factors Must Be Capped In Order to Determine Physical 
Depreciation 
 
As described previously, the current ORPTS trend and depreciation methodology increases the 
market value of assets that remain in service well beyond their published service lives.  By establishing a 
cap to the HW trend factors at 95 percent of their service lives, the resulting market value for those 
assets remaining in service longer than their service lives will remain constant year-on-year. 
 
The caps for the various service lives are computed by multiplying the respective service lives by 
95 percent to establish the cap year.  The HW index is adjusted by setting the index of the years 
following the cap year equal to the index for the cap year.  As a result, any assets with in-service dates 
greater than the cap year will be trended with the same trend as the cap year and depreciated to 95 
percent of the indicated ReproCN, stabilizing the market value of these assets and, thus, removing the 
             Appendix A 
       Page 19 of 107


--- Page 29 ---
17 
 
contradictory increase in value ascribed to antiquated and obsolete assets that is present in the 
methodology currently employed by ORPTS. 
C. 
Calculation of Functional Obsolescence Due to Excess Capital Costs Associated With 
Changes in Technology - Cast Iron versus Plastic Pipe 
 
The functional obsolescence adjustment was calculated by comparing the current construction 
costs for various types of material present in the Company’s system.  Using RS Means data, a cost per 
linear foot was developed for various pipe diameters for plastic (polyethylene), cast-iron, and steel 
(schedule 40).  The cost ratio between the most cost-effective materials to the actual material was 
calculated to determine a “percent good.”  This “percent good” takes into consideration the functional 
obsolescence due to excess capital costs inherent in the pipe.  The “percent depreciated” is simply the 
“percent good” subtracted from 100%.  The appropriate “percent depreciated” was then matched to 
each distribution and services entity based on the average pipe size and pipe material in the entity.  
Lastly, the “percent depreciated” was weighted based on the mileage of each pipe material to develop 
an all-encompassing percent functional obsolescence. 
 
The following methodology was utilized to develop the functional obsolescence due to excess 
capital costs for the Company’s gas distribution mains and services: 
 
Using RS Means cost information, current construction costs for plastic (polyethylene), cast-iron, 
and steel (schedule 40) were compiled; this included materials, labor, and trenching.  RS Means, 
a division of Reed Construction Data, is the leading supplier of North American construction cost 
information, providing accurate and current cost data used to estimate different construction 
cost possibilities.  It is well respected in the construction industry, making it suitable for 
replacement cost estimates. 
 
A table of costs per linear foot for pipe diameters between one inch and 24 inches was 
composed.  Dependent on the size of the pipe, plastic or steel has cost advantages over other 
materials.  At a diameter of eight inches or less, plastic costs less than steel or cast-iron; 
however, at a diameter of greater than eight inches, steel costs less than plastic or cast-iron. 
 
The following cost ratios were calculated: plastic to cast-iron and plastic to steel for pipe 
diameters four inches or smaller, and steel to cast iron and steel to plastic for pipe diameters 
larger than four inches. 
 
To determine the functional obsolescence penalty due to excess capital costs, the cost ratios 
were subtracted from 100%. 
 
The next step in the analysis was to determine the average pipe size for the Company’s 
distribution and services pipe.  Note: The transmission pipe is constructed of steel, which is the 
optimal material for the larger diameters of the transmission mains; thus, no functional 
obsolescence is applied. 
 
The weighted average pipe size was calculated from the 2016 DOT Reports, which provides the 
miles of mains in the system detailed by size and material. 
             Appendix A 
       Page 20 of 107


--- Page 30 ---
18 
 
The functional obsolescence percentage corresponding to the pipe size and material was then weighted 
based on the mileage of each material in the system.   A resulting penalty was determined which must 
be applied to the pipeline assets for gas distribution mains and services. 
D. 
Calculation of Economic Obsolescence 
 
 
The economic obsolescence valuation methodology included with this Request begins with the 
Reproduction Cost New (“RCN”) of the utility assets.  The RCN is calculated by trending the historical 
costs of the assets being appraised.  Next, all identified forms of physical depreciation and functional 
obsolescence have been deducted from RCN.  This calculation results in Reproduction Cost New Less 
Physical Depreciation and Functional Obsolescence.   This figure is then adjusted by the addition of Total 
Working Capital, which consists of 1/7 of Net Operating Expense, Materials and Supplies, Gas Storage 
Underground, Liquefied Natural Gas, and Prepayments.  Next, the calculation is adjusted through the 
deduction of Construction Work in Progress (CWIP).  A further adjustment is made by adding Minor 
Projects to the figure.  Lastly, Additional Adjustments to Rate Base are added to the calculation in order 
to account for regulatory assets that contribute to earnings.  These adjustments are made to 
Reproduction Cost New Less Physical Depreciation and Functional Obsolescence so that the Achieved 
Rate of Return (see below) is properly calculated by accounting for all of the assets that contribute to or 
support earnings.  
 
The ratio of Net Operating Income (“NOI”) to Reproduction Cost New Less Physical Depreciation 
and Functional Obsolescence is calculated by dividing NOI into Reproduction Cost New Less Physical 
Depreciation and Functional Obsolescence and is labeled the “Achieved Return Rate.”   
 
The Weighted Average Cost of Capital (“WACC”) was derived from the U.S. natural gas 
distribution industry.  A copy of the WACC study is included in this Request.  See Exhibit C.  The achieved 
return rate is compared to the current year WACC.  To the extent that the Achieved Return Rate falls 
short of the WACC, economic obsolescence is present.  Dividing the Achieved Return Rate by the WACC 
yields a percent good.  The inverse of the percent good figure is the economic obsolescence percentage 
or factor.  Multiplying the derived economic obsolescence factor by the “Reproduction Cost New Less 
Physical Depreciation and Functional Obsolescence” yields the total dollar amount of economic 
obsolescence.  Once the economic obsolescence figure is deducted from Reproduction Cost New Less 
Physical Depreciation and Functional Obsolescence, the resulting figure is the Cost Indicator of Value.  
See Exhibit D. 
VI.

ADDENDUM• Handy Whitman Revision Methodology
The rationale behind any adjustments to the Handy Whitman index is based on the premise that
excess capital costs are being captured in the indices due to the lack of re-weighting of the historical
figures. This condition carries significance, as was evident by the conduit comparisons between the AUS
Telephone Plant index and the Handy Whitman Index (Exhibit A). The acceptance of the AUS
Telephone Plant index as a tool to calculate Reproduction Cost New, as is the case in the State of New
York, offers a inherent willingness to factor in any modernization adjustments that can potentially be
quantified on a trend index. Our goal was to offer a similar adjustment on the Handy Whitman index by
developing a revised series of trend tables for the affected years.
The analysis and revision of the Handy Wbitman trend factors was perfonried off the Gas and
Electric Utility Indices for Region I (North Atlantic) as of July 1,2010. Data was obtained by purchase
of Bulletin No. 172 through Whitman Requardt & Associates, the publishers of the index. For relevance
with the subject property the analysis was limited to Transmission and Distribution accounts as shown
below:

To maximize accuracy the analysis was extended to the entire range of the Index: annual figures
from 1912 to 1997 and semiannual figures from 1998 to 2010. Using these date ranges as our initial
basis, the analysis began by identifying labor and material indices most relevant to each account. For
purposes of this analysis we chose to model only materials and labor components. Equipment costs were
not factored into the analysis. which we felt was appropriate given today’s pervasive use of specialty
contractor services. Specifically, equipment indices (prices for excavators, trenchers, etc) would have to
be included in any analysis if we assumed modem standards dictated that utilities buy, operate, and
service the vast majority of equipment used to construct the operating plant. While this may have been
the case when the Handy Whitman indices were first developed, and while ownership of specialized
equipment may still be evident in some limited cases, our assumption is that contractors today will
handle the ownership and operation of the equipment and offer it to utilities as a service during
construction. The resulting need for these services would be captured in our labor weightings.
Across all accounts, we chose to measure the labor component using the Consumer Price Index
-
All Urban Consumers for the New York/New Jersey region. The Consumer Price Index, or CPI. is one
of several price indices calculated by various national statistical agencies. Not only is the percent change
in the CPI a measure estimating inflation, but the CPI can also be used, among other things, to adjust for
the effect of inflation on money wages, salaries. pensions. and other labor costs. We selected this index
for two reasons: first, the index captured specific regional adjustments that other national labor price
indices could not and second, this index provided the earliest available index figures, dating back
tol 915. Other typical labor indices such as the Producer Price Index either fall short of this timeframe or
do not reflect a regional adjustment, let alone a state-specific adjustment which our Regional CPI does.
Again, the goal is to model Index values older than 1973, thus the labor component we select must
capture both the timeframe and the regional adjustment.
For the materials components. we chose to use Handy Whitman’s own published Materials
index, also provided by Whitman Requardt & Associates in Bulletin No. 172, This source was chosen
for two reasons. First, the Materials Index is published in the same format as the Gas and Electric Utility
Indices
— all of which date back to 1912, with series broken out into the same annual and semiannual
time periods. Second, we felt inclusion of Handy Whitman data added more relevance to the analysis.
since presumably these materials are the same ones used in Handy Whitman’s own semiannual
calculations of index figures. Notably, the Handy Whitman Materials index does not contain a regional
price adjustment, which we felt was appropriate given that the labor index we chose already captures
this variation.
For selection of which materials to apply to each account in the analysis, we relied upon several
sources:
(1) The FERC account descriptions of components that comprise each account, as well as the
Handy Whitman mapping of FERC account to Handy Whitman account
(2) National Grid’s expertise and familiarity with the subject assets
(3) Duff& Phelps’ own engineering expertise in the gas and electric utility industries
             Appendix A 
       Page 25 of 107


--- Page 35 ---
As mentioned previously, for each account in the analysis, the original index (the dependent
variable) and the labor and material(s) indices (the independent variables) were all normalized based on
their respective July 2009 published price. The equation chosen to model these normalized figures
would resemble the following for any given time period t:
Y(t) = \VeightIh1,, x CPIJahor(t) + \Veightrnatcrjai A x Pricenjtcjai A(t)
Accounts with multiple materials would contain additional factors for Material B, C, etc. To
minimize statistical error we limited the total number of regressed variables to four- three material
indices and the labor index. In most cases however either one or two materials was the norm.
From here, a multiple regression analysis was conducted on the independent variables (materials
and labor) against the dependent variable (the normalized cost index). Regressions were conducted
across three time regions:
(I) Figures from 1915-1973’
(2) Figures 1973 to July 2009
(3) All years (1915 to July 2009)
To prevent unwanted variability we chose to set the y-intercept of each regression to zero, which
would allow the pricing of materials and labor to thIly capture the variation in the formula. Results of
each set of regressions were plotted on an Analysis of Variance (ANOVA) table. One such example, the
post-1973 regression for Gas Distribution Mains using the Regional CPI index as the labor variable and
Handy Whitman Steel Pipe as the material variable, is shown below:
‘The 1915 starting point was selected because the labor Index selected did not provide figures older than
1915.

The critical statistics to note are as follows:
•
Coefficients
— the weightings on materials and labor which fonn this index;
•
Adjusted R Square
— the proportion of variability in a data set that Is accounted for by the
statistical model. Values range from 0 (no variability captured in the model) to I (variability
is filly captured In the model);
•
F Score
— the ratio of explanatory power of predictors used in the model relative to the
unused predictors. Values range from I (average predictor Is no better than a random
number) to infinity (average predictor explains a lot of the index relative to a random
number).
•
P-value of the coefficient
— primitively simplified as: the probability that the relationship
between this variable and the dependent variable doesn’t actually exist. Values range from 0
(no probability of irrelevance, i.e. VERY relevant) to I (variable is irrelevant).
The results indicate that for the post-I 973 period, the normalized CPI Regional index carries a
weight of 0.696, while the Handy Whitman Steel Distribution Pipe carries a weight of 0.380. Put another
way, for any time period from 1973 to 2009, the Handy Whitman Steel Distribution Mains Index can be
estimated by summing 69.6% of that year’s Regional CPI figure with 38.0% of that year’s Steel Mains
price. The analysis in this case indicates we have devised a very accurate model of the index over this
time period, given the model’s Adjusted R square (over 97.8%), very large F score (78,837), and very
small P-values for each of the independent variables. These results are consistent with statements from
Whitman Requardt & Associates Indicating weightings have generally not been updated in over 40-50
years.
             Appendix A 
       Page 27 of 107


--- Page 37 ---
Results for every regression in the post-1973 period were scrutinized carefully and evaluated
against high inclusion standards. First, results were evaluated for a minimum adjusted R square of 95%.
Additionally, all results were checked for a high F score to determine the fitness of the overall model.
Finally, each predictor (independent) variable in the analysis was evaluated for a minimum p-value of
0.2; anything larger was seemed statistically insignificant and discarded. We employed an iterative
approach when any of these conditions failed; regression would be repeated until all these conditions
were met. Our results were consistently held to be statistically accurate as summarized below:

--- Page 38 ---
Using these results, the pre-1973 Indices could now be rebuilt by using the post-1973 weightings
and applying them to the pre-1973 prices for labor and materials. The resulting index would be a revised
AND normalized index for each account.
The final step in the process was to un-normalize the new index and compute the proper index
figures that should be used in place of the published pre-1973 figures. The revised un-normalized Handy
Whitman indices for Gas Utility (Region 1) and Electric Utility (Region I) for 1912 through 1972 can
be found below.
It is important to note for purposes of this analysis, the post-I 973 index figures were left
unchanged. Given construction standards have changed rapidly since 1973, it is plausible that even the
index weightings used here are not appropriate today’s standards given modern construction
Improvements and efficiencies developed in the past 40 years. At this time however we believe the
approach of using the post-1973 weightings provides the closest proxy of modem standards available to
us at this moment. Any prior or fUture analysis developed which demonstrates adjustments are
warranted in the past 40-50 years would not overlap the analysis conducted here, but may be used in
fUture revisions to the overall index as needed.

FOREWORD
Tradition of Quality
The
Handy-Whitman
Index
of
Public
Utility
Construction Costs has been published continuously
since 1924. Formerly the Handy Index, Bulletin Nos, I
through 15 were developed by William W. Handy of
Baltimore who had wide valuation experience in public
utilities. He believed that valuation studies should not
be confined to rate cases but should be kept alive to the
benefit of the utility indusuy.
He began publishing
index numbers for electric and gas construction cost
trends. Carrying on with the tradition ofquality, after
Mr. Handy=s death, we continued publication for his
estate beginning with Bulletin 16.
Then, January 1,
1950,
Whitman,
Requardt
and
Associates,
LLP
purchased rights to the publication and have since been
the sole publishers.
The name Al-tandy-Whitman Index® was adopted for
Bulletin No. 53 and succeeding issues to combine the
names of Mr. Handy and Ezra B. Whitman, a well-
known valuation engineer. In 1957 an index of water
utility construction costs was added.
Mr. Whitman
was a consultant on the publication of the Index until
his death in 1963.
Whitman, Requardt and Associates, LLP
Ezra B. Whitman, a well-known valuation engineer
was one of the founders of our firm. Major Whitman,
as he was known from his World War I service, had
already made a name for himself.
Prior to the
founding ofthe firm in 1915, Major Whitman had been
President and Chief Engineer ofthe Water Board ofthe
City of Baltimore.
He designed the first rapid sand
filtration plant serving a major city while he was the
Baltimore Water Engineer.
He was also president of
the American Society of Civil Engineers and of the
American Institute of Consulting Engineers and a
chairman of the
Public Service
Commission of
Maryland.
The Handy-Whitman Index is prepared especially for
electric, gas and water utilities and is the only known
publication of its kind available to the public. The list
of subscribers is international and includes operating
utilities,
regulatory
bodies,
valuation
engineers,
equipment
industries,
insurance
companies
and
reference libraries.
Tradition of Quality Continued
Since 1915, Whitman, Requardt and Associates, LLP,
has been an independent consulting engineering firm
organized to serve government, industry and private
enterprise.
The
firm
has
steadily expanded
its
engineering
capabilities, providing complete services for civil,
sanitary,
structural,
mechanical
and
electrical
engineering
and
architectural
projects
from job
inception
through
construction
management.
Construction cost data from utility projects ofall types
are available from design and valuation assignuments,
The staff is composed ofspeciaiists in these and related
disciplines who bring a diverse professional and
academic expertise to each assignment. A full-time
staff is maintained specifically for preparing the
Handy-Whitman Index.
Methods of Preparation of Indexes
An index number is a percentage ratio between the cost
of an item at any stated time and its cost at a base
period, or:
cost at stated time
Tndex Number = cost at base period
X 100
Index numbers have been prepared for many items)
including wage rates, cost-of-living, material and
equipment costs, and financial transactions.
In the
Handy-Whitman Index, index numbers have been
developed for ABuilding Construction®, ?Flectric
Utility Construction®, AGas Utility Construction® and
AWater Utility
Construction®.
Prices
of basic
materials such as cement, sand, gravel, cast iron pipe,
wire, etc., are obtained from publications such as
Engineering News-Record and checked against prices
actually being paid for such materials.
Labor cost
trends are computed from labor rates obtained from
sources such as the Construction Labor Research
Council.
Prices and cost trends of equipment are
obtained from nationally recognized manufacturers,
and operating utilities.
Handy-Whitman Index numbers are developed from
wage rates and prices prevailing on January I and July
I each year. The index numbers are generally based on
1973 = 100, although those items of recent origin are
based on a later year.
The proportions of basic materials, labor, equipment
and other cost components used in the Handy-Whitnan
Index
are
based
on
analyses
developed during
valuation and design assignments and on data
furnished by utilities and industrial sources willing to
assist with the Index.
These data are reviewed
continuously, and weightings and components are
revised as required.
This review assures that the
indexes published reflect current construction practice.
111
             Appendix A 
       Page 36 of 107


--- Page 46 ---
FOREWORD
Geographic Regions
To reflect differing cost tends throughout the 48
contiguous states, the index has been divided into six
geographical regions of similar characteristics. They
are shown on the accompanying map.
Use of Index Numbers
Handy-Whitman Index numbers have been widely used
to tend earlier valuations and original cost records to
estimate reproduction cost at prices prevailing at a
certain date.
The use of indexes for an appropriate
property item or group will provide a reliable guide to
changes in cost.
Cost tends are given for all the
important items of property.
The electric and gas
groups are arranged by the Federal Energy Regulatory
Commission Uniform System ofAccounts. The water
property
accounts
are
arranged
to
follow
the
classification ofthe National Association ofRegulatory
Utility Commissioners and the American Water Works
Association.
The Handy-Whitman Index will furnish a yardstick for
the fluctuations in value of property which will be
satisfactory for many purposes. In rate cases, when a
more exact determination ofvalue is desired, however,
the Index must be used carefully. Average prices and
cost trends are used to develop the Index, and any
direct application of cost trends without checking with
actual local experience may not be accepted without
controversy. When local experience is compared with
the index and the correlation between the two trends is
determined, the result is satisfactory. Costs tended by
such a method are used to assist in establishing a rate
base.
Indexes in these bulletins are used to trend earlier
valuations or original cost records for insurance
purposes.
The Handy-Whitman Index has a general application in
valuations of all types of property.
The building
construction cost trends may be used wherever similar
items of property are to be compared.
Many of the
other tends may be used for related items in other
industries because of their similarity.
State-of-the-art
changes
often
affect
costs
independently of inflation.
New regulatory and
environmental requirements, changes in work rules and
improved design standards, for instance, increase
construction costs even though the price of wages,
materials and equipment may be static.
Trended
construction costs will not reflect such changes.
However, n-ended costs are a reasonably accurate
measure of the cost of reproducing actual plant.
Although every effort is made to maintain accuracy,
Whitman, Requardt and Associates, LU’ disclaim any
responsibility for the use of these indexes, because
local conditions may vary.
No guarantee or warranty of any kind is made in the
sale ofthe Handy-Whitman Index. Published numbers
are occasionally subject to change based upon receipt
of new or different information. These numbers will
be bolded.
Further inquiries on electric, gas and water indexes
should be addressed to Whitman, Requardt and
Associates, LLP.
Total Electric Plant and Function
Three indexes are provided for total plant. The first is
for all steam generation and the other two for weighted
combinations of steam and nuclear, and steam and
hydro generation. Indexes are also provided for each
function.
Indexes
are
not
maintained
for
plani
accounts
323,324,325,341,345
and 346.
We believe that
indexes for comparable accounts in other functions are
sufficiently accurate for these accounts.
The indexes for total nuclear production and total other
production incorporate comparable indexes from the
steam production function for the accounts not listed.
Value of Index Numbers
We believe that present-day reproduction cost of any
property can be calculated more accurately using index
numbers than by repricing a complcte inventory.
Trending the controlling items ofproperty in any utility
by the index method saves time and effort in arriving at
a valuation. Analyzing and determining cost trends for
all of the great numbers of articles of plant that
represent only a very small proportion of the value of
the utility is not necessary. They may be assumed to
follow in general the trend ofthe controlling items, and
the fluctuations in value above or below the tends of
the controlling items will tend to offset each other and
have a very slight effect on the total value.
Comments on Bulletin No. 172
During the twelve month period ending July 1, 2010,
the average index ofall geographical regions for Total
Gas Plant increased 4.6% and the comparable index for
Electric Plant-All Steam Generation increased 5.2%.
November 2010
Whitman, Rcquardt and Associates, LEP

SUBJECT:
Property Tax Mitigation Accelerate Program. 
Request: 
Note: In all interrogatories, all requests for workpapers or supporting calculations shall be 
construed as requesting any Word, Excel or other computer spreadsheet models in 
original electronic format with all formulae intact and unlocked. 
Starting on p. 17 of the testimony of the Shared Services Panel, the Companies forecast an 
annual reduction of $2.5 million for property tax assessment reductions expected to begin with 
the 2020 assessment roll. 
1. 
Provide the total reduction amount the consultant proposed for potential areas of property 
tax reductions across National Grid’s US operating Companies. 
2. 
When does the Company believe the programming will be completed to identify the 
nontaxable cost components, for both KEDNY and KEDLI, and to follow through and 
remove them from reportable asset base? 
3. 
Explain how the Company calculate the $2.5 million in annual savings, for both KEDNY 
and KEDLI, through this mitigation accelerate program.  Provide the supporting 
calculations. 
4. 
Reconcile the amounts listed in the Companies’ Exhibit__(RRP-11), Schedule 1, 
workpaper 7, for KEDNY, and Exhibit__(RRP-11), Schedule 1, workpaper 6, for 
KEDLI, with the $19.1 million amount stated on the Shared Services Panel testimony on 
p. 23, ln 7. 
Response: 
 
1.
The consultant’s projected Year 1 savings were approximately $31.19 million across all 
National Grid operating companies before project costs.  The New England portion of the 
             Appendix A  
       Page 101 of 107


--- Page 111 ---
Form 103 
initiative was implemented prior to the Company’s Accelerate Program.  Projected 
savings for New York are $10 million ($2.5 million for each of KEDNY and KEDLI), as 
shown in Exhibit______(RRP-11), Workpapers to Exhibit_____(RRP-3), Schedule 34, 
Workpaper 1.  
2.
The programming has been completed and the initial proposal for the valuation reduction 
was submitted to the New York Office of Real Property Tax Services (“ORPTS”) for 
their review and consideration as part of the Companies’ April 30th filing.  The 
Companies expect to meet with representatives from ORPTS in July or August to begin 
discussions regarding the proposed valuation reduction.   
3.
The total savings were projected using a broad average tax rate.  The Companies 
allocated the savings to KEDNY and KEDLI based on the plant cost used in the 
consultants work as shown below: 
Line 
Amount
1

$20,916,513,785 
2

$2,667,003,500

12.75%
4

$31,185,404

$3,976,360

$1,023,640

Total estimated savings (Line 5 + Line 6) 
$5,000,000 
The savings on Line 7 were split evenly between KEDNY and KEDLI. 
4.
Attachment 1 presents the total property tax savings included in the property tax forecast 
of $18.1 million over the term of the proposed rate plan.  The statement indicating $19.1 
million in the Shared Services Panel testimony was a typographical error that should have 
stated $18.1 million.

SECTION 1: INVESTMENT DESCRIPTION 
 
 
Problem Statement and Current Situation 
National Grid’s legacy Customer Information Systems (CIS’) were implemented decades 
ago and are quickly approaching obsolescence. The case for change is driven by the 
company’s need to address systemic infrastructure challenges it currently faces and the 
need to meet complex requirements of new customer programs, emerging digital 
technologies, and progressive rate design. An evolving energy marketplace and customer 
expectations regarding their service experience demand a significantly enhanced technology 
platform – i.e., one that is designed to meet the information needs of customers in the 
current and future environment and one that will support policy goals related to improved 
customer choice, deployment of advanced technologies and increased reliance on 
distributed energy resources. Replacement of key business and billing processes with the 
proposed CIS solution will produce many benefits enabling National Grid to more effectively 
implement new customer programs and rate options and providing customers with a 
significantly improved customer-centric service experience. Modernization of National Grid’s 
CIS systems will transform the way the company does business and elevate the service and 
support customers receive in the evolving energy market for decades to come. Initiating the 
transition to a modern CIS platform must begin now given the current state of the legacy CIS 
systems and the timeline required for replacement. 
 
Specific systemic infrastructure challenges identified as part of the customer strategy 
assessment leading to this business case evaluation include: 
 
Delivery Risk:  
National Grid’s legacy customer systems are less stable and less reliable due, in part, to 
technology obsolescence, a rapid deployment of increasingly complex rates and other 
regulatory requirements, and an exponential increase in the amount of data processing 
required by the system since the legacy systems were deployed. Also contributing to the 
instability is the number and complexity of integrations between the legacy CIS systems and 
subsystems that have been added over two decades as the energy market, regulatory 
requirements and customer-utility relationship have transformed. Current customer systems 
are increasingly difficult to adapt to the needs of the new digitally equipped customer and to 
regulatory and company demands that they were never prepared to serve (e.g. new 
products and services, virtual net metering, etc.). 
 
Operational Risk:  
Technologies used in current systems are largely unsupported and required skillsets are 
difficult to find in the current market (e.g. imminent retirement of key resources). In addition, 
there is a wide range of system-driven operational and technology pain points. For example, 
the business is unable to keep up with new Distributed Generation requirements due to 
system and resource limitations. A lack of business-configurable capabilities drives 
significant dependence on IT for even simple business rule and configuration value changes 
creating unnecessary lags in deploying such changes (e.g. rate changes). In the area of 
Retail Choice, there are numerous manual-intensive workarounds in place that could be 
resolved through systems, data, and process improvement. With 6.7M bills per month 
rendered by CRIS and CSS, and 12.8M customer calls per year, these pain points must be 
addressed. 
 


--- Page 121 ---
Exhibit __ (SSP-5CU) 
Page 4 of 51 
 
 
 
Business Risk:  
A digital consumer technology revolution is underway and its profound force presents both 
challenges and opportunities for the utility industry. The rapid pace of change is accelerating 
market convergence, reducing barriers for new market entrants, driving down the cost of 
product innovation, and increasing the speed at which consumers adopt new technologies. 
Smart and distributed technologies (such as home energy management, solar, electric 
vehicle, and storage), mobile applications, and data-driven products and services are 
coming to market and evolving quickly. Such drivers and National Grid’s emerging offerings 
(e.g. Distributed Generation, Retail Choice, NG Ventures, EV) require business agility to 
bring them to market and quickly operationalize in a manner that currently is not achievable 
due to limitations of the current infrastructure. 
 
To facilitate this alignment, National Grid is progressing its offer of digitally-enabled 
customer service capabilities required to maintain exemplary relationships with its customers 
and developing new products and services that will provide a differentiated value 
proposition.  However, the customer facing capabilities to be enabled through programs 
such as GBE and CxT require the core CIS systems to keep pace with a customer-central, 
digital-first customer service delivery model. 
This new delivery model requires stability of the systems of record (i.e. CIS) and continuous 
innovation and differentiation for the systems of engagement (i.e. Salesforce CRM). 
Emerging offerings will also require the company to develop new skills and technology, 
create and strengthen strategic partnerships, and refine its program and project governance 
structure. 
 
 
 
 


--- Page 122 ---
Exhibit __ (SSP-5CU) 
Page 5 of 51 
 
 
Program Outcome – What Does Success Look Like? 
 
While National Grid is proud of its past success in providing accurate bills and excellent 
customer service, doing so has come at a cost given the age and limited capabilities of its 
current CIS systems. The enhanced services provided to customers have been achieved 
through highly customized functions in the legacy CIS systems and the addition of sub-
systems in the legacy landscape. These modifications greatly increase the complexity of the 
customer service and revenue cycle application portfolio, adding functional redundancy and 
creating data proliferation in various databases and systems where customer information 
resides. The lack of a consolidated system that provides a 360-degree view of the customer 
creates obstacles to providing a consistent and positive customer experience. In addition, 
“layering” on of customized programming necessary to implement regulatory requirements 
over the years results in a highly complicated system in which each new, approved 
regulatory change requires inordinate time and resources to implement and is difficult to 
scale or adapt for each operating company. 
 
The goal of this program is to replace the current legacy CIS systems (CSS, CRIS) with a 
modern, flexible application capable of performing critical meter-to-cash processes with 
greater efficiency, scalability, and extensibility for future products and services. This program 
will also seek to complete the realization of customer service capabilities as set forward in 
National Grid’s Customer Strategy requiring augmentation of the Salesforce CRM platform 
and integration services currently being deployed by the Gas Business Enablement (GBE) 
program. This program will also refactor the integration services needed to enable the digital 
customer experiences that form part of this Customer Strategy. 
 
When the CIS Program has been implemented, success will look like this: 
 
• The Customer Experience when interacting with National Grid is effortless thanks to 
increased choices and flexibility 
• A new Customer Information System provides leading, flexible, and scalable 
capabilities setting up National Grid for the future 
• National Grid can deliver lower cost revenue cycle services (meter reading, billing, 
credit & collections, payments) while enabled to grow and flex with customer, 
business and regulator emerging needs (e.g. new products & services), Distributed 
Generation, Energy Efficiency, and Demand Response programs 
• The new CIS allows employees to deliver revenue cycle services with fewer 
exceptions and manual workarounds to customers, getting it right the first time 
 
 
Risks and Opportunities 
 
There is a clear opportunity for a modern CIS to establish the foundation required to grow 
National Grid’s business while reducing cost to serve and mitigate the delivery, operational, 
and business risks the company faces today.  However, as with any large and complex 
program, there are risks to consider with taking on the implementation. Primary risks to 
consider when embarking on a CIS implementation include: 
 


--- Page 123 ---
Exhibit __ (SSP-5CU) 
Page 6 of 51 
 
 
1)  Other in-flight programs such as Gas Business Enablement (GBE), Customer 
Experience Transformation (CxT) and Advanced Meter Infrastructure (AMI) introduce 
interdependencies with the new CIS platform to enable full benefit realization.   
 
2)  The CIS replacement alone will not generate cost savings to cover implementation 
costs, so regulatory recovery is critical to the financial success of this program. 
 
3)  Risk of limited resource availability among National Grid staff due to demand across 
several large programs running in parallel. 
 
 
 
Section 8 includes a detailed list of risks and mitigation plans 
 
 
Solution Strategy  
The proposed program to replace National Grid’s Customer Information System 
infrastructure will include the following activities: 
 
• Implement core CIS functions on future state platform for all customer types and 
jurisdictions 
• Integrate the core CIS platform with the retained systems and vendors 
• Integrate the core CIS with the CxT, GBE, AMI, and other project solutions where 
needed 
• Enhance / extend the CRM for functionality that is enabled by the new core CIS where 
needed
